\mathcal{H}
contains no unknown entrants; thus
\mathcal{H}
is terminal. As a result, it holds that
\textsc{Reg}(\textsc{ExploreOne};\mathcal{H})=0
by Lemma
C.1
and
\textsc{Reg}(\textsc{EFA};\mathcal{H})=0
by Lemma
B.1
.

Induction hypothesis.

Suppose that
\textsc{Reg}(\textsc{ExploreOne};\mathcal{H}^{\prime})\leq c\textsc{Reg}(\textsc{EFA};\mathcal{H}^{\prime})
for any history
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
with at most
k
unknown entrants.

Induction Step.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a history with
k+1
unknown entrants. If
\mathcal{H}
is terminal, then
\textsc{Reg}(\textsc{ExploreOne};\mathcal{H})=0
by Lemma
C.1
and
\textsc{Reg}(\textsc{EFA};\mathcal{H})=0
by Lemma
B.1
. Suppose
\mathcal{H}
is not terminal. Let
Z
be the first round at which an unknown entrant is purchased under
ExploreOne
if it starts from
\mathcal{H}
. Given that an unknown entrant is purchased with probability at least
\frac{h(\mathcal{F})}{(c+1)w_{\text{max}}}
where
w_{\text{max}}=\max\{w_{i}(\mathcal{H}):i\in\mathcal{N}\cup\{0\}\}
, then
\expect[Z]\leq\frac{(c+1)w_{\text{max}}}{h(\mathcal{F})}
. As a result,
{\mathbb{P}}[Z<\infty]=1
. Thus, the regret of
ExploreOne
is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\textsc{Reg}(\textsc{ExploreOne};\mathcal{H})=\textsc{EpochReg}(S^{\star}_{(c-1)}(\mathcal{H})\cup U_{1};\mathcal{H})

\displaystyle\hskip 102.43008pt+\expect\limits_{\mathcal{H}_{Z},S_{Z},Y_{Z},w_{Y_{Z}}}[\textsc{Reg}(\textsc{ExploreOne};\mathcal{H}_{Z}\cup(S_{Z},Y_{Z},w_{Y_{Z}}))|\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{H}]

\displaystyle\leq\textsc{EpochReg}(S^{\star}_{(c-1)}(\mathcal{H})\cup U_{1};\mathcal{H})+c\cdot\expect\limits_{\mathcal{H}_{Z},S_{Z},Y_{Z},w_{Y_{Z}}}[\textsc{Reg}(\textsc{EFA};\mathcal{H}_{Z}\cup(S_{Z},Y_{Z},w_{Y_{Z}}))|\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{H}]

\displaystyle=\textsc{EpochReg}(S^{\star}_{(c-1)}(\mathcal{H})\cup U_{1};\mathcal{H})+c\cdot\expect\limits_{w\sim\mathcal{F}}[\textsc{Reg}(\textsc{EFA},J(\mathcal{H})\cup\{w\})]

\displaystyle\leq c\cdot\left(\min\limits_{S\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})}\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{H})+\expect\limits_{w\sim\mathcal{F}}[\textsc{Reg}(\textsc{EFA},J(\mathcal{H})\cup\{w\})]\right)=c\cdot\textsc{Reg}(\textsc{EFA};\mathcal{H}).

The first equality uses
Z<\infty
almost surely and decomposes the regret of
ExploreOne
into the first epoch regret and the future regret. The first inequality uses
\textsc{Reg}(\textsc{ExploreOne};\mathcal{H}_{Z}\cup(S_{Z},Y_{Z},w_{Y_{Z}}))\leq\textsc{Reg}(\textsc{EFA};\mathcal{H}_{Z}\cup(S_{Z},Y_{Z},w_{Y_{Z}}))
by the induction hypothesis as
\mathcal{H}_{Z}\cup(S_{Z},Y_{Z},w_{Y_{Z}})
has
k
unknown entrants. The second equality uses
\textsc{Reg}(\textsc{EFA};\mathcal{H}_{Z}\cup(S_{Z},Y_{Z},w_{Y_{Z}}))=\textsc{Reg}(\textsc{EFA},J(\mathcal{H})\cup\{w_{Y}\})
by Lemma
B.2
, noting that the set of known products’ attraction parameters is augmented with
w_{Y}
after the entrant’s purchase, and that
w_{Y}\sim\mathcal{F}
. The second inequality uses
\textsc{EpochReg}(S^{\star}_{(c-1)}(\mathcal{H})\cup U_{1};\mathcal{H})\leq c\min\limits_{S\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})}\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{H})
by Lemma
C.11
. The last equality uses Lemma
B.3
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
C.11
.

To prove the lemma, we first derive an expression for the epoch regret of an arbitrary assortment
S=S^{K}\cup U_{\ell^{\prime}}
consisting of
\ell^{\prime}
unknown entrants and a subset of known products
S^{K}
. Any known product
i\in S^{K}
is purchased
\frac{w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}{\ell^{\prime}h(\mathcal{F})}
rounds in expectation yielding a regret of
\textsc{OPT}-1
per round. The outside option is purchased
\frac{w_{0}}{\ell^{\prime}h(\mathcal{F})}
rounds in expectation yielding a regret of
OPT
per round. An unknown entrant is purchased once at the end of the epoch yielding regret
\textsc{OPT}-1
. Hence, the epoch regret of
S
is

\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{H})=\sum_{i\in S^{K}}\frac{w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}{\ell^{\prime}h(\mathcal{F})}(\textsc{OPT}-1)+\frac{w_{0}}{\ell^{\prime}h(\mathcal{F})}\textsc{OPT}+(\textsc{OPT}-1).

(35)

Letting
\ell^{\star}\in\{1,\ldots,c\}
be the optimal number of unknown entrants explored by
EFA
,
EFA
offers assortment
S^{\star}_{(c-\ell^{\star})}(\mathcal{H})\cup U_{\ell^{\star}}
during the epoch. As a result, the
c
-scaled epoch regret of
EFA
is lower bounded by

\displaystyle c\cdot\min\limits_{S\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})}\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{H})=c\cdot\textsc{EpochReg}(S^{\star}_{(c-\ell^{\star})}(\mathcal{H})\cup U_{\ell^{\star}};\mathcal{H})\geq\ell^{\star}\cdot\textsc{EpochReg}(S^{\star}_{(c-\ell^{\star})}(\mathcal{H})\cup U_{\ell^{\star}};\mathcal{H})

\displaystyle=\ell^{\star}\cdot\left(\sum_{k=1}^{c-\ell^{\star}}\frac{w_{(k)}(\mathcal{H})}{\ell^{\star}h(\mathcal{F})}(\textsc{OPT}-1)+\frac{w_{0}}{\ell^{\star}h(\mathcal{F})}\textsc{OPT}+(\textsc{OPT}-1)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{c-\ell^{\star}}\frac{w_{(k)}(\mathcal{H})}{h(\mathcal{F})}(\textsc{OPT}-1)+\frac{w_{0}}{h(\mathcal{F})}\textsc{OPT}+(\ell^{\star}-1)(\textsc{OPT}-1)+(\textsc{OPT}-1)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{k=1}^{c-1}\frac{w_{(k)}(\mathcal{H})}{h(\mathcal{F})}(\textsc{OPT}-1)+\frac{w_{0}}{h(\mathcal{F})}\textsc{OPT}+(\textsc{OPT}-1)=\textsc{EpochReg}(S^{\star}_{(c-1)}(\mathcal{H})\cup U_{1};\mathcal{H}).

The first equality uses
\min\limits_{S\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})}\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{H})=\textsc{EpochReg}(S^{\star}_{(c-\ell^{\star})}(\mathcal{H})\cup U_{\ell^{\star}};\mathcal{H})
as
EFA
minimizes epoch regret. The first inequality uses that
c\geq\ell^{\star}
and the the epoch regret is non-negative. The second equality uses (
35
) for
S=S^{\star}_{(c-\ell^{\star})}(\mathcal{H})\cup U_{\ell^{\star}}
. The second inequality uses that
(\ell^{\star}-1)(\textsc{OPT}-1)\geq\sum_{k=c-\ell^{\star}+1}^{c}\frac{w_{(k)}(\mathcal{H})}{h(\mathcal{F})}(\textsc{OPT}-1)
as
\frac{w_{(k)}(\mathcal{H})}{h(\mathcal{F})}\geq 1
(since
w^{\star}_{(c)}(\mathcal{I}_{1})\geq h(\mathcal{F})
) and
\textsc{OPT}-1<0
. The last equality uses (
35
) for
S=S^{\star}_{(c-1)}(\mathcal{H})\cup U_{1}
. This completes the lemma proof.
∎

Appendix D
Heterogeneous rewards (Section
6.1
)

D.1
Heterogeneous rewards and a single entrant (Theorem
6.1
)

We use the next lemma which is the analogue of Lemma
3.1
in this heterogeneous-rewards model.

Lemma D.1
.

For any instance with
\textsc{OPT}_{1}>\textsc{Rev}^{\star}_{1}
, there exists an optimal policy which offers the entrant with a fixed set of incumbents until the entrant is purchased and offers the revenue-maximizing assortment in subsequent rounds.

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

For a set
S
of at most
c-1
incumbents let
\tau(S)
be the expected number of rounds until the entrant is purchased if the platform uses
\pi(S)
. Letting
N_{i}
be the number of rounds option
i\in S\cup\{0\}
is purchased, the expectation of
N_{i}
is

\expect[N_{i}]={\mathbb{P}}[\text{i is purchased}|S]\tau(S)=\frac{{\mathbb{P}}[\text{i is purchased}|S]}{{\mathbb{P}}[\text{entrant is purchased}|S]}=\frac{\frac{w_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S}w_{i}+h(\mathcal{F})+w_{0}}}{\frac{h(\mathcal{F})}{\sum_{i\in S}w_{i}+h(\mathcal{F})+w_{0}}}=\frac{w_{i}}{h(\mathcal{F})}.

(36)

We refer to the set of rounds up to and including the purchase of the entrant as the
exploration period
.
For any product
i\in S
,
\pi(S)
incurs a regret of
\textsc{OPT}_{1}-r_{i}
during the exploration period and this happens
\expect[N_{i}]
rounds in expectation. Furthermore,
\pi(S)
incurs regret of
\textsc{OPT}_{1}
every time the outside option is purchased and this happens
\expect[N_{0}]
rounds in expectation. Moreover,
\pi(S)
incurs a regret of
\textsc{OPT}_{1}-r
when the unknown entrant is purchased. As a result, the regret of
\pi(S)
equals

\displaystyle\textsc{Reg}(\pi(S))

\displaystyle=\expect\Bigg[\sum_{i\in S}(\textsc{OPT}_{1}-r_{i})N_{i}+\textsc{OPT}_{1}N_{0}+(\textsc{OPT}_{1}-r)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in S}(\textsc{OPT}_{1}-r_{i})\expect[N_{i}]+\textsc{OPT}_{1}\expect[N_{0}]+(\textsc{OPT}_{1}-r)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in S}(\textsc{OPT}_{1}-r_{i})\frac{w_{i}}{h(\mathcal{F})}+\textsc{OPT}_{1}\frac{w_{0}}{h(\mathcal{F})}+(\textsc{OPT}_{1}-r)

\displaystyle=\frac{w_{0}}{h(\mathcal{F})}\sum_{i\in S}\textsc{SIR}_{i}(1)+\textsc{OPT}_{1}\frac{w_{0}}{h(\mathcal{F})}+(\textsc{OPT}_{1}-r)

(37)

where the first equality uses that
\pi(S)
incurs zero regret after the exploration period, the third equality uses (
36
) and the fourth equality uses
(\textsc{OPT}_{1}-r_{i})\frac{w_{i}}{h(\mathcal{F})}=\frac{w_{0}}{h(\mathcal{F})}\textsc{SIR}_{i}(1)
by the definition of
\textsc{SIR}_{i}(1)
. By (
D.1
) minimizing
\textsc{Reg}(\pi(S))
is equivalent to minimizing
\sum_{i\in S}\textsc{SIR}_{i}(1)
which is achieved exactly when
S
consists of the
\min(c-1,n^{<0}_{1})
incumbents with the lowest scaled interim regrets, i.e.,
S=S_{1}
.

Thus,
\pi(S_{1})
yields a lower regret than any other policy
\pi(S)
. Given that there exists an optimal policy which is
\pi(S)
for some
S
by Lemma
D.1
,
\pi(S_{1})
is optimal.
∎

Proof of Lemma
D.1
.

The lemma is a special case of Lemma
D.2
(stated in Appendix
D.2
and proven in Appendix
D.3
) by taking
\pi^{\star}
to offer a fixed
S^{\prime}\in\argmin_{S\in\mathcal{E}_{t}}\textsc{EpochReg}_{t}(S)
until the entrant is purchased.
∎

D.2
Optimal policy under different rewards and multiple entrants (Theorem
6.2
)

The proof follows a similar structure to the proof of Theorem
4.1
. Let
\mathcal{E}_{t}=\{S\subseteq\mathcal{N}:|S|\leq c,S\cap(\mathcal{N}\setminus\mathcal{I}_{t})\neq\emptyset\}
be the set of assortments containing at least one entrant at round
t
. For a set
S\in\mathcal{E}_{t}
, let
\tau(S;\mathcal{H}_{t})
be the expected number of rounds and
r(S;\mathcal{H}_{t})
be the expected total reward up to and including the first round an entrant is purchased if
S
is offered repeatedly. Let
\textsc{EpochReg}_{t}(S)=\textsc{OPT}_{t}\tau(S;\mathcal{H}_{t})-r(S;\mathcal{H}_{t})
be the expected regret until the first purchase of an unknown entrant if
S\in\mathcal{E}_{t}
is offered repeatedly. We refer to the set of rounds between two consecutive unknown entrant purchases as an
epoch
.

The following lemma (which is the analogue of Lemma
4.1
and is proven in Appendix
D.3
) shows that any policy which minimizes regret within each epoch is optimal.

Lemma D.2
.

A policy
\pi^{\star}
is optimal if for any round
t
and history
\mathcal{H}_{t}
, it satisfies

•

\pi^{\star}(\mathcal{H}_{t})\in\argmin_{S\in\mathcal{E}_{t}}\textsc{EpochReg}_{t}(S)

if

\textsc{OPT}_{t}>\textsc{Rev}^{\star}_{t}

•

\pi^{\star}(\mathcal{H}_{t})=S^{\star}_{t,\textsc{known}}
otherwise

The next lemma (which is the analogue of Lemma
4.2
and is proven in Appendix
D.6
) establishes that conditioned on including
\ell
unknown entrants, complementing them the
\min(c-\ell,n_{t}^{<0})
known entrants with the lowest scaled interim regrets minimizes the regret in the current epoch.

Lemma D.3
.

For any round
t
and any
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,k_{t}\}
, the assortment
S
that minimizes
\textsc{EpochReg}_{t}(S)
subject to including exactly
\ell
unknown products
U_{\ell}
complements them with the set
V^{\star}_{(\min(c-\ell,n_{t}^{<0}))}(\mathcal{I}_{t})
of the
\min(c-\ell,n_{t}^{<0})
products with lowest scaled interim regrets, i.e.,

V^{\star}_{(\min(c-\ell,n_{t}^{<0}))}(\mathcal{I}_{t})\in\argmin\limits_{S^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{t},|S^{\prime}|\leq c-\ell}\textsc{EpochReg}_{t}(U_{\ell}\cup S^{\prime}).

The following lemma (which is the analogue of Lemma
4.3
and is proven in Appendix
D.7
) establishes that the optimal number of unknown entrants to explore is the maximum between the largest
\ell
such that
\textsc{OPT}_{t}\geq\beta_{t}(\ell)
and
c-n_{t}^{<0}
.

Lemma D.4
.

For any round
t
, the number of unknown entrants
\ell_{t}
selected by Algorithm
2
minimizes
\textsc{EpochReg}_{t}(U_{\ell}\cup V^{\star}_{(\min(c-\ell,n_{t}^{<0}))}(\mathcal{I}_{t}))
subject to
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,k_{t}\}
.

Proof of Theorem
6.2
.

By Lemma
D.2
a policy is optimal if it minimizes within-epoch regret when it is worth it to explore and maximizes revenue otherwise. HEFA maximizes revenue when it is not worth it to explore (line
8
, Algorithm
2
) and by Lemmas
D.3
and
D.4
it minimizes within-epoch regret when it is worth it to explore (line
4
, Algorithm
2
). Thus, HEFA is optimal.
∎

D.3
Epoch-regret-minimizing policies are optimal (Proof of Lemma
D.2
)

As in the proof of Lemma
4.1
, Lemma
B.1
, Lemma
B.2
, Lemma
B.3
, and Lemma
B.4
continue to hold in this setting. The proofs of Lemma
B.2
, Lemma
B.3
, and Lemma
B.4
remain unchanged. The proof of Lemma
B.1
needs a small modifications and it is provided in Appendix
D.4
. Instead of Lemma
B.5
, we show the following lemma (proof in Appendix
D.5
).

Lemma D.5
.

For any history
\mathcal{H}
,
\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H})\geq-m\cdot r
and
\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H})<\infty
.

Proof of Lemma
D.2
.

The proof combines Lemma
B.1
, Lemma
B.2
, Lemma
B.3
, Lemma
B.4
, and Lemma
D.5
and uses the exact same steps as the proof of Lemma
4.1
, where Lemma
B.5
is replaced with Lemma
D.5
. In the case when the expected length of the first epoch is infinite (Case 1,
\expect[Z]=\infty
), we replace “Lemma
B.5
implies that
\expect_{w\sim\mathcal{F}}[\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star},J(\mathcal{H})\cup\{w\})]{\mathbb{P}}[Z<\infty]\geq-m
” with “Lemma
D.5
implies that
\expect_{w\sim\mathcal{F}}[\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star},J(\mathcal{H})\cup\{w\})]{\mathbb{P}}[Z<\infty]\geq-m\cdot r
”.
∎

D.4
Any policy has non-negative regret given a terminal history (Lemma
B.1
)

Updated proof of Lemma
B.1
.

Lemma
B.6
and Lemma
B.7
continue to hold in this setting and the proof of Lemma
B.1
is completed in the same way as in Section
B.3
. The proof of Lemma
B.6
remains unchanged. The proof of Lemma
B.7
needs a modification in inequality (
B.3
), and otherwise remains unchanged. In (
B.3
) we need to show that
\expect[\textsc{OPT}|\mathcal{E}^{\geq h}]>\textsc{OPT}(\mathcal{H})
under the assumption that there exists some
S^{\prime}
with at least one unknown entrant such that
\textsc{Rev}(S^{\prime};\mathcal{H})>\textsc{OPT}(\mathcal{H})
. To show this, let
S^{K}
be the set of known products in
S^{\prime}
given
\mathcal{H}
. The revenue of
S^{\prime}
is at least the revenue of
S^{K}
:

\displaystyle\textsc{Rev}(S^{\prime};\mathcal{H})

\displaystyle=\frac{r\cdot\ell h(\mathcal{F})+\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}r_{i}}{\ell h(\mathcal{F})+\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}+1}>\textsc{OPT}(\mathcal{H})=\max\limits_{S\subseteq I(\mathcal{H}),|S|\leq c}\textsc{Rev}(S;\mathcal{H})

\displaystyle\geq\textsc{Rev}(S^{K};\mathcal{H})=\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}r_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}+1}.

(38)

The first equality uses the expression for the expected revenue of
S^{\prime}
and the first inequality uses the assumption that
\textsc{Rev}(S^{\prime};\mathcal{H})>\textsc{OPT}(\mathcal{H})
. The second equality uses that
\mathcal{H}
is terminal and the second inequality uses that
S^{K}
is a set of known products. The third equality uses the expression for the expected revenue of
S^{K}
. As a result of (
D.4
) the reward of each entrant is greater than the revenue of
S^{K}
:

\frac{r\cdot\ell h(\mathcal{F})+\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}r_{i}}{\ell h(\mathcal{F})+\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}+1}>\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}r_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}+1}\iff r>\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}r_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}+1}

(39)

Then, the conditional expected ex-post optimum
\expect[\textsc{OPT}|\mathcal{E}^{\geq h}]
is greater than
\textsc{OPT}(\mathcal{H})
:

\displaystyle\expect[\textsc{OPT}|\mathcal{E}^{\geq h}]

\displaystyle\geq\expect\Bigg[\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}}w_{i}r_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}}w_{i}+1}\Bigg|\mathcal{E}^{\geq h}\Bigg]=\expect\Bigg[\frac{r\cdot\sum_{i\in A_{\ell}}w_{i}+\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}r_{i}}{\sum_{i\in A_{\ell}}w_{i}+\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}+1}\Bigg|\mathcal{E}^{\geq h}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{r\cdot\ell h(\mathcal{F})+\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}r_{i}}{\ell h(\mathcal{F})+\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}+1}=\textsc{Rev}(S^{\prime};\mathcal{H})>\textsc{OPT}(\mathcal{H}).

The second inequality uses that
\sum_{i\in A_{\ell}}w_{i}\geq\ell h(\mathcal{F})
(due to
\mathcal{E}^{\geq h}
) and that the function
f(x)\coloneqq\frac{r\cdot x+\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}r_{i}}{x+\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}+1}
is increasing since its derivative is positive

f^{\prime}(x)=\frac{r\cdot(\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}+1)-\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}r_{i}}{(\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}+1)^{2}}=(\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}+1)\frac{r-\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}r_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}+1}}{(\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}+1)^{2}}>0

where the last inequality uses (
39
).
∎

D.5
Optimal policy regret is bounded independently of the history (Lemma
D.5
)

To prove Lemma
D.5
the following lemma (proven at the end of this subsection) lower bounds the epoch regret of any assortment and any history with the entrant’s reward.

Lemma D.6
.

For any non-terminal history
\mathcal{H}
, and set
S\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})
,
\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{H})\geq-r
.

The next lemma upper bounds the epoch regret with the epoch regret by the maximum reward multiplied by the maximum epoch length. Let
r_{\text{max}}=\max\{\{r_{i}:i\in\{m+1,\ldots,n\}\}\cup\{r\}\}
be the maximum reward.

Lemma D.7
.

For any non-terminal history
\mathcal{H}
, and set
S\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})
,
\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{H})\leq r_{\text{max}}\left(1+\frac{\sum_{i\in I(\mathcal{H})}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+1}{h(\mathcal{F})}\right)
.

Proof of Lemma
D.5
.

We show by induction on
k
that for any history
\mathcal{H}
with
k
unknown entrants remaining,

\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H})\geq-k\cdot r\quad\text{ and }\quad\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H})<r_{\text{max}}\left(k+\frac{k\Big(\sum_{i\in I(\mathcal{H})}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+k\expect_{w\sim\mathcal{F}}[w]+1\Big)}{h(\mathcal{F})}\right)

(40)

Base of induction.

If
k=0
,
\mathcal{H}
is terminal and Lemma
B.1
implies that
\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H})=0
.

Induction step.

Suppose (
40
) holds for
k
and we will show it for
k+1
. If
\mathcal{H}
is terminal, then by Lemma
B.1
,
\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H})=0
which satisfies the conditions. Suppose
\mathcal{H}
is not terminal. Then by Lemma
B.3
,

\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H})=\min\limits_{S\subseteq\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})}\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{H})+\underbrace{\expect[\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H}_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}}\cup(S_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}},Y_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}},w_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}}))|\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{H}]}_{(\star)}.

(41)

Given that
(\star)\geq-k\cdot r
by the induction hypothesis and
\min\limits_{S\subseteq\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})}\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{H})\geq-r
by Lemma
D.6
, the lower bound of (
40
) follows. Letting
\mu\coloneq\expect_{w\sim\mathcal{F}}[w]
and

W(\mathcal{H})\coloneq\sum_{i\in I(\mathcal{H})}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})
as shorthands, the regret of
\pi^{\star}
given
\mathcal{H}
is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H})

\displaystyle=\min\limits_{S\subseteq\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})}\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{H})+\expect[\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H}_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}}\cup(S_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}},Y_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}},w_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}}))|\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{H}]

\displaystyle\leq\min\limits_{S\subseteq\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})}\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{H})+r_{\text{max}}\expect_{w_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}}\sim\mathcal{F}}\Bigg[k+\frac{k\Big(W(\mathcal{H})+w_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}}+k\mu+1\Big)}{h(\mathcal{F})}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq r_{\text{max}}\left(1+\frac{W(\mathcal{H})+1}{h(\mathcal{F})}\right)+r_{\text{max}}\expect_{w_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}}\sim\mathcal{F}}\Bigg[k+\frac{k\Big(W(\mathcal{H})+w_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}}+k\mu+1\Big)}{h(\mathcal{F})}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq r_{\text{max}}\left(k+1+\frac{(k+1)\Big(W(\mathcal{H})+(k+1)\mu+1\Big)}{h(\mathcal{F})}\right).

The first inequality uses the induction hypothesis; the second inequality uses Lemma
D.7
; the third equality uses
\expect_{w_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}}\sim\mathcal{F}}[w_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}}]=\mu\geq 0
. This yields the upper bound in (
40
) and finishes the induction and the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
D.6
.

The epoch regret of
S
is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{H})=\Bigg(\textsc{OPT}(\mathcal{H})-\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}r_{i}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+r\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+1}\Bigg)\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+1}{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle\geq\Bigg(\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}r_{i}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+1}-\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}r_{i}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+r\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+1}\Bigg)\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+1}{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Big(\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}r_{i}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})\Big)\Big(\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+1\Big)}{\Big(\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})\Big)\Big(\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+1\Big)}-\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}r_{i}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}-r

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}r_{i}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}\Bigg(1+\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+1}\Bigg)-\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}r_{i}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}-r\geq-r.

The first equality expresses the epoch regret as the regret per round of
S
multiplied by the expected number of rounds in an epoch. The first inequality uses
\textsc{OPT}(\mathcal{H})\geq\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}r_{i}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+1}
. The second equality multiplies each term in the brackets by
\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+1}{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}
, cancels the attraction parameter sum
\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+1
from the denominator of the second term and separates
r
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
D.7
.

The epoch regret is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{H})

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\textsc{OPT}(\mathcal{H})-\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}r_{i}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+r\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+1}\Bigg)\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+1}{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle\leq r_{\text{max}}\left(\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+1}{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}\right)\leq r_{\text{max}}\left(1+\frac{\sum_{i\in I(\mathcal{H})}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+1}{h(\mathcal{F})}\right).

The first equality expresses the epoch regret as the regret per round of
S
multiplied by the expected number of rounds in an epoch. The first inequality upper bounds the regret per round by
\textsc{OPT}(\mathcal{H})-\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}r_{i}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+r\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+1}\leq r_{\text{max}}
as
\textsc{OPT}(\mathcal{H})\leq r_{\text{max}}
and
\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}r_{i}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+r\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+1}\geq 0
. The second inequality uses that
\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{k}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})\leq\sum_{i\in I(\mathcal{H})}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})
(as
S^{\textsc{K}}\subseteq I(\mathcal{H})
) and
\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})\geq h(\mathcal{F})
.
∎

D.6
Optimal completion to set of unknown entrants (Lemma
D.3
)

To prove Lemma
D.3
, the following lemma provides an expression for the epoch regret of an assortment offering
\ell
unknown entrants. Recall that
m_{t}
is the number of unknown entrants left and that
k_{t}=\min(c,m_{t})
.

Lemma D.8
.

For any
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,k_{t}\}
and any assortment
S^{\prime}
of at most
c-\ell
known products,

\textsc{EpochReg}_{t}(U_{\ell}\cup S^{\prime})=\frac{w_{0}}{\ell h(\mathcal{F})}\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}}\textsc{SIR}_{i}(t)+\textsc{OPT}_{t}\frac{w_{0}}{\ell h(\mathcal{F})}+(\textsc{OPT}_{t}-r)

Proof of Lemma
D.3
.

By Lemma
D.8
, minimizing
\textsc{EpochReg}_{t}(U_{\ell}\cup S^{\prime})
is equivalent to minimizing the term
\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}}\textsc{SIR}_{i}(t)
which is achieved exactly when
S^{\prime}
consists of the
\min(c-\ell,n_{t}^{<0})
known products with the lowest scaled interim regrets, i.e.,
S=V^{\star}_{(\min(c-\ell,n_{t}^{<0}))}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
D.8
.

Letting
N_{i}
be the number of rounds option
i\in S^{\prime}\cup\{0\}
is chosen during the current epoch if
U_{\ell}\cup S^{\prime}
is offered,

\expect[N_{i}]={\mathbb{P}}[\text{$i$ is purchased}|U_{\ell}\cup S^{\prime}]\tau(U_{\ell}\cup S^{\prime};\mathcal{I}_{t})=\frac{\frac{w_{i}}{\sum_{j\in S^{\prime}}w_{j}+\ell h(\mathcal{F})+w_{0}}}{\frac{\ell h(\mathcal{F})}{\sum_{j\in S^{\prime}}w_{j}+\ell h(\mathcal{F})+w_{0}}}=\frac{w_{i}}{\ell h(\mathcal{F})}.

(42)

For any product
i\in S
,
\pi(S)
incurs a regret of
\textsc{OPT}_{t}-r_{i}
during the epoch and this happens
\expect[N_{i}]
rounds in expectation. Furthermore,
\pi(S)
incurs regret of
\textsc{OPT}_{t}
every time the outside option is purchased and this happens
\expect[N_{0}]
rounds in expectation. Moreover,
\pi(S)
incurs a regret of
\textsc{OPT}_{t}-r
when the unknown entrant is purchased. Thus the epoch regret equals

\displaystyle\textsc{EpochReg}_{t}(U_{\ell}\cup S^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\expect\Bigg[\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}}(\textsc{OPT}_{t}-r_{i})N_{i}+\textsc{OPT}_{1}+(\textsc{OPT}_{t}-r)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}}(\textsc{OPT}_{t}-r_{i})\expect[N_{i}]+\textsc{OPT}_{t}\expect[N_{0}]+(\textsc{OPT}_{t}-r)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}}(\textsc{OPT}_{t}-r_{i})\frac{w_{i}}{\ell h(\mathcal{F})}+\textsc{OPT}_{t}\frac{w_{0}}{\ell h(\mathcal{F})}+(\textsc{OPT}_{t}-r)

\displaystyle=\frac{w_{0}}{\ell h(\mathcal{F})}\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}}\textsc{SIR}_{i}(t)+\textsc{OPT}_{t}\frac{w_{0}}{\ell h(\mathcal{F})}+(\textsc{OPT}_{t}-r)

where the third equality uses (
42
) and the fourth equality uses
(\textsc{OPT}_{t}-r_{i})\frac{w_{i}}{\ell h(\mathcal{F})}=\frac{w_{0}}{\ell h(\mathcal{F})}\textsc{SIR}_{i}(t)
.
∎

D.7
Optimal number of unknown entrants (Lemma
D.4
)

To prove Lemma
D.4
, let
\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell)=\textsc{EpochReg}_{t}(U_{\ell}\cup V^{\star}_{(\min(c-\ell,n_{t}^{<0}))})
for ease of notation. The following lemma compares
\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell)
and
\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell+1)
when
\ell\geq c-n_{t}^{<0}
. This is the analogue of (
4.3
) and Lemma
4.4
.

Lemma D.9
.

For any
\ell\geq c-n_{t}^{<0}
,
\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell)\geq\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell+1)
if and only if
\textsc{OPT}_{t}\geq\beta_{t}(\ell+1)

Given that known products with positive scaled interim regret are not included, exploring less than
c-n_{t}^{<0}
unknown entrants is never optimal. This is formalized in the next lemma.

Lemma D.10
.

For any round
t
with
\textsc{OPT}_{t}>\textsc{Rev}^{\star}_{t}
and number of unknown entrants
\ell<c-n_{t}^{<0}
,
\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell)>\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(c-n_{t}^{<0})
.

Proof of Lemma
D.4
.

To prove the lemma it suffices to show that

\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell)\geq\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell_{t}^{\star})\text{ for all $\ell\in\{1,\ldots,k_{t}\}$}

Let
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,k_{t}\}
. We consider cases based on whether
\ell\geq\ell_{t}^{\star}
,
\ell_{t}^{\star}>\ell\geq c-n_{t}^{<0}
, or
\ell<c-n_{t}^{<0}
.

Case 1:
\ell\geq\ell_{t}^{\star}
.

For any
\ell^{\prime}\geq\ell_{t}^{\star}
, it holds that
\ell^{\prime}\geq\ell_{t}
as
\ell_{t}^{\star}=\max(\ell_{t},c-n_{t}^{<0})
. As a result,
\textsc{OPT}_{t}<\beta(\ell^{\prime}+1)
and Lemma
D.9
yields
\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell^{\prime}+1)\geq\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell^{\prime})
. Applying this for
\ell^{\prime}\in\{\ell-1,\ldots,\ell_{t}^{\star}\}
yields
\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell)\geq\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell_{t}^{\star})
.

Case 2:
\ell_{t}^{\star}>\ell\geq c-n_{t}^{<0}
.

Given that
\ell_{t}^{\star}=\max(\ell_{t},c-n_{t}^{<0})
, this case is only possible when
\ell_{t}^{\star}=\ell_{t}
. For any
\ell^{\prime}
such that
\ell_{t}>\ell^{\prime}
and
\ell^{\prime}\geq c-n_{t}^{<0}
,
\textsc{OPT}_{t}\geq\beta(\ell^{\prime}+1)
, and Lemma
D.9
yields
\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell^{\prime}+1)\leq\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell^{\prime})
. Applying this for
\ell^{\prime}\in\{\ell,\ldots,\ell_{t}-1\}
yields

\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell)\geq\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell_{t})=\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell_{t}^{\star}).

Case 3:
\ell<c-n_{t}^{<0}
.

Then

\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell)>\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(c-n_{t}^{<0})\geq\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell_{t}^{\star})

where the first inequality uses Lemma
D.10
and the second inequality uses the previous case.
∎

Proof of Lemma
D.9
.

For any
\ell^{\prime}\geq c-n_{t}^{<0}
, the epoch regret is expressed as

\displaystyle\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\textsc{EpochReg}_{t}(U_{\ell^{\prime}}\cup V^{\star}_{(\min(c-\ell^{\prime},n_{t}^{<0}))})=\textsc{EpochReg}_{t}(U_{\ell^{\prime}}\cup V^{\star}_{(c-\ell^{\prime})})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{c-\ell^{\prime}}w_{0}\textsc{SIR}_{(i)}^{\star}(t)+w_{0}\textsc{OPT}_{t}}{\ell^{\prime}h(\mathcal{F})}+\textsc{OPT}_{t}-r.

(43)

The second equality uses
\min(c-\ell^{\prime},n_{t}^{<0})=c-\ell^{\prime}
(as
c-\ell^{\prime}\leq n_{t}^{<0}
). The third equality uses Lemma
D.8
. Using (
D.7
) for
\ell^{\prime}\in\{\ell,\ell+1\}
, the second term cancels;
\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell)\geq\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell+1)
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{c-\ell}w_{0}\textsc{SIR}_{(i)}^{\star}(t)+w_{0}\textsc{OPT}_{t}}{\ell h(\mathcal{F})}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{c-\ell-1}w_{0}\textsc{SIR}_{(i)}^{\star}(t)+w_{0}\textsc{OPT}_{t}}{(\ell+1)h(\mathcal{F})}

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{c-\ell}\textsc{SIR}_{(i)}^{\star}(t)+\textsc{OPT}_{t}}{\ell}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{c-\ell-1}\textsc{SIR}_{(i)}^{\star}(t)+\textsc{OPT}_{t}}{(\ell+1)}

\displaystyle\iff(\ell+1)\sum_{i=1}^{c-\ell}\textsc{SIR}_{(i)}^{\star}(t)+(\ell+1)\textsc{OPT}_{t}

\displaystyle\geq\ell\sum_{i=1}^{c-\ell-1}\textsc{SIR}_{(i)}^{\star}(t)+\ell\textsc{OPT}_{t}

\displaystyle\iff\textsc{OPT}_{t}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{c-\ell-1}-\textsc{SIR}_{(i)}^{\star}(t)-(\ell+1)\textsc{SIR}_{(c-\ell)}^{\star}(t)=\beta_{t}(\ell+1)

∎

Proof of Lemma
D.10
.

For any
\ell^{\prime}\leq c-n_{t}^{<0}
, the epoch regret is expressed as

\displaystyle\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\textsc{EpochReg}_{t}(U_{\ell^{\prime}}\cup V^{\star}_{(\min(c-\ell^{\prime},n_{t}^{<0}))})=\textsc{EpochReg}_{t}(U_{\ell^{\prime}}\cup V^{\star}_{(n_{t}^{<0})})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t}^{<0}}w_{0}\textsc{SIR}_{(i)}^{\star}(t)+w_{0}\textsc{OPT}_{t}}{\ell^{\prime}h(\mathcal{F})}+\textsc{OPT}_{t}-r.

(44)

The second equality uses
c-\ell^{\prime}\geq n_{t}^{<0}
(as
\ell^{\prime}\leq c-n_{t}^{<0}
); the third equality uses Lemma
D.8
.
Applying (
D.7
) for
\ell^{\prime}\in\{c-n_{t}^{<0},\ell\}
, the second term cancels and
\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(c-n_{t}^{<0})<\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}(\ell)
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t}^{<0}}w_{0}\textsc{SIR}_{(i)}^{\star}(t)+w_{0}\textsc{OPT}_{t}}{(c-n_{t}^{<0})h(\mathcal{F})}

\displaystyle<\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t}^{<0}}w_{0}\textsc{SIR}_{(i)}^{\star}(t)+w_{0}\textsc{OPT}_{t}}{\ell h(\mathcal{F})}

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t}^{<0}}\textsc{SIR}_{(i)}^{\star}(t)+\textsc{OPT}_{t}}{(c-n_{t}^{<0})}

\displaystyle<\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t}^{<0}}\textsc{SIR}_{(i)}^{\star}(t)+\textsc{OPT}_{t}}{\ell}

\displaystyle\iff\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t}^{<0}}\textsc{SIR}_{(i)}^{\star}(t)+\textsc{OPT}_{t}>0

\displaystyle\iff\textsc{OPT}_{t}>\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t}^{<0}}-\textsc{SIR}_{(i)}^{\star}(t)

\displaystyle\iff\textsc{OPT}_{t}>\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t}^{<0}}(r_{(i)}-\textsc{OPT}_{t})\frac{w_{(i)}}{w_{0}}

\displaystyle\iff\textsc{OPT}_{t}>\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t}^{<0}}r_{(i)}w_{(i)}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t}^{<0}}w_{(i)}+w_{0}}

(45)

The third equivalence uses that
\frac{x}{\ell}<\frac{x}{c-n_{t}^{<0}}
if and only if
x>0
(as
\ell<c-n_{t}^{<0}
) for
x=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{t}^{<0}}\textsc{SIR}_{(i)}^{\star}(t)+\textsc{OPT}_{t}
.

We conclude the proof by showing (
D.7
):

\textsc{OPT}_{t}>\textsc{Rev}^{\star}_{t}
and
\textsc{Rev}^{\star}_{t}
is the maximum revenue from known products, thus greater than the revenue from known products with negative scaled interim regrets.
∎

D.8
Connection to homogeneous-rewards case (Section
6.1
)

Proposition D.1
.

For any instance where
r_{i}=1
,
\textsc{OPT}_{t}\geq\beta_{t}(\ell)
if and only if
\textsc{OPT}_{t}\geq\alpha_{t}(\ell)

Proof of Proposition
D.1
.

\displaystyle\textsc{OPT}_{t}

\displaystyle\geq\beta_{t}(\ell)\iff\textsc{OPT}_{t}\geq-\sum_{i=1}^{c-\ell}\textsc{SIR}_{(i)}(t)-\ell\cdot\textsc{SIR}_{(c-\ell+1)}(t)

\displaystyle\iff\textsc{OPT}_{t}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{c-\ell}(1-\textsc{OPT}_{t})\frac{w^{\star}_{(i)}(\mathcal{I}_{t})}{w_{0}}-\ell\cdot(1-\textsc{OPT}_{t})\frac{w^{\star}_{(c-\ell+1)}(\mathcal{I}_{t})}{w_{0}}

\displaystyle\iff\textsc{OPT}_{t}

\displaystyle\Bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{c-\ell}w^{\star}_{(i)}(\mathcal{I}_{t})+\ell w^{\star}_{(c-\ell+1)}(\mathcal{I}_{t})+w_{0}\Bigg)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{c-\ell}w^{\star}_{(i)}(\mathcal{I}_{t})+\ell w^{\star}_{(c-\ell+1)}(\mathcal{I}_{t})

\displaystyle\iff\textsc{OPT}_{t}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{c-\ell}w^{\star}_{(i)}(\mathcal{I}_{t})+\ell w^{\star}_{(c-\ell+1)}(\mathcal{I}_{t})}{\sum_{i=1}^{c-\ell}w^{\star}_{(i)}(\mathcal{I}_{t})+\ell w^{\star}_{(c-\ell+1)}(\mathcal{I}_{t})+w_{0}}\iff\textsc{OPT}_{t}\geq\alpha_{t}(\ell)

∎

Appendix E
Heterogeneous priors (Section
6.2
)

E.1
Characterization of the optimal policy with two entrants (Theorem
6.3
)

We prove the following theorem which is an extension of Theorem
6.3
. This characterizes the exact regret differences between each two of the three exploration policies
\pi^{1}
,
\pi^{2}
, and
\pi^{1,2}
and the theorem follows directly subsequently. We include this lemma as it helps make the proofs of Propositions
6.1
and
6.2
more concise.

Lemma E.1
.

For any instance in the class
\mathcal{C}
, letting
h_{i}=h(\mathcal{F}_{i})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
the regret differences are

\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1,2})-\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1})=\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\Bigg[\Big(\frac{h_{2}}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\Big(\frac{\max(w_{2},w_{3})}{h_{1}}-\frac{\max(w_{1},w_{3})}{h_{2}}\Big)-\frac{w_{3}}{h_{1}}\Big)(\textsc{OPT}-1)-\frac{1}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\textsc{OPT}\Bigg],

\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1,2})-\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{2})=\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\Bigg[\Big(\frac{h_{1}}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\Big(\frac{\max(w_{1},w_{3})}{h_{2}}-\frac{\max(w_{2},w_{3})}{h_{1}}\Big)-\frac{w_{3}}{h_{2}}\Big)(\textsc{OPT}-1)-\frac{1}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\textsc{OPT}\Bigg],

\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{2})-\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1})=\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\left[\left(\frac{w_{3}}{h_{1}}+\frac{\max(w_{2},w_{3})}{h_{2}}\right)(\textsc{OPT}-1)-\left(\frac{w_{3}}{h_{2}}+\frac{\max(w_{1},w_{3})}{h_{1}}\right)(\textsc{OPT}-1)\right].

Proof of Theorem
6.3
.

The proof follows directly by Lemma
E.1
.
∎

To prove Lemma
E.1
the next lemmas (proven in Section
E.2
) characterize the regret of
\pi^{1}
,
\pi^{2}
, and
\pi^{1,2}
.

Lemma E.2
.

The regret of
\pi^{1}
,
\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1})
, equals

\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\left[\left(\frac{w_{3}}{h_{1}}+\frac{\max(w_{1},w_{3})}{h_{2}}\right)(\textsc{OPT}-1)+\left(\frac{1}{h_{1}}+\frac{1}{h_{2}}\right)\textsc{OPT}+2(\textsc{OPT}-1)\right].

Lemma E.3
.

The regret of
\pi^{2}
,
\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{2})
, equals

\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\left[\left(\frac{w_{3}}{h_{2}}+\frac{\max(w_{2},w_{3})}{h_{1}}\right)(\textsc{OPT}-1)+\left(\frac{1}{h_{1}}+\frac{1}{h_{2}}\right)\textsc{OPT}+2(\textsc{OPT}-1)\right].

Lemma E.4
.

The regret of
\pi^{1,2}
,
\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1,2})
equals

\displaystyle\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\Bigg[

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\left(\frac{h_{1}\max(w_{1},w_{3})}{h_{2}}+\frac{h_{2}\max(w_{2},w_{3})}{h_{1}}\right)(\textsc{OPT}-1)+\frac{1+\nicefrac{{h_{1}}}{{h_{2}}}+\nicefrac{{h_{2}}}{{h_{1}}}}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\textsc{OPT}+2(\textsc{OPT}-1)\Bigg].

Proof of Lemma
E.1
.

Subtracting the expression of Lemma
E.2
from the one of Lemma
E.4
and rearranging terms, the difference between
\pi^{1,2}
and
\pi^{1}
equals

\displaystyle\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1,2})-\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1})

\displaystyle=\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\Bigg[\Big(\frac{h_{2}}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\Big(\frac{\max(w_{2},w_{3})}{h_{1}}-\frac{\max(w_{1},w_{3})}{h_{2}}\Big)-\frac{w_{3}}{h_{1}}\Big)(\textsc{OPT}-1)

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{1}{h_{1}}+\frac{1}{h_{2}}-\frac{1}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\left(1+\frac{h_{1}}{h_{2}}+\frac{h_{2}}{h_{1}}\right)\right)\textsc{OPT}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\Bigg[\Big(\frac{h_{2}}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\Big(\frac{\max(w_{2},w_{3})}{h_{1}}-\frac{\max(w_{1},w_{3})}{h_{2}}\Big)-\frac{w_{3}}{h_{1}}\Big)(\textsc{OPT}-1)-\frac{1}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\textsc{OPT}\Bigg].

The first equality separately groups all terms with
\textsc{OPT}-1
and
OPT
. The last equality uses the identity
\frac{1}{h_{1}}+\frac{1}{h_{2}}-\frac{1}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\left(1+\frac{h_{1}}{h_{2}}+\frac{h_{2}}{h_{1}}\right)=\frac{1}{h_{1}+h_{2}}
by Lemma
E.5
(stated and proven right below). Due to an analogous argument replacing
\pi^{1}
with
\pi^{2}
and using Lemma
E.3
instead of Lemma
E.2
,

\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1,2})-\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{2})=\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\Bigg[\Big(\frac{h_{1}}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\Big(\frac{\max(w_{1},w_{3})}{h_{2}}-\frac{\max(w_{2},w_{3})}{h_{1}}\Big)-\frac{w_{3}}{h_{2}}\Big)(\textsc{OPT}-1)-\frac{1}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\textsc{OPT}\Bigg].

Subtracting the expressions of Lemma
E.3
and Lemma
E.2
, the regret difference between
\pi^{2}
and
\pi^{1}
is

\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{2})-\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1})=\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\Bigg[\Bigg(\frac{w_{3}}{h_{1}}+\frac{\max(w_{2},w_{3})}{h_{2}}\Bigg)(\textsc{OPT}-1)-\Bigg(\frac{w_{3}}{h_{2}}+\frac{\max(w_{1},w_{3})}{h_{1}}\Bigg)(\textsc{OPT}-1)\Bigg].

∎

Lemma E.5
.

For any
h_{1}>0
and
h_{2}>0
,

\frac{1}{h_{1}}+\frac{1}{h_{2}}-\frac{1}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\left(1+\frac{h_{1}}{h_{2}}+\frac{h_{2}}{h_{1}}\right)=\frac{1}{h_{1}+h_{2}}
.

Proof.

Rearranging terms and factoring out
\frac{1}{h_{1}}
and
\frac{1}{h_{2}}
, the left-hand side is expressed as

\frac{1}{h_{1}}\Big(1-\frac{h_{2}}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\Big)+\frac{1}{h_{2}}\Big(1-\frac{h_{1}}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\Big)-\frac{1}{h_{1}+h_{2}}=\frac{1}{h_{1}}\frac{h_{1}}{h_{1}+h_{2}}+\frac{1}{h_{2}}\frac{h_{2}}{h_{1}+h_{2}}-\frac{1}{h_{1}+h_{2}}=\frac{1}{h_{1}+h_{2}}.

∎

E.2
Regret characterization of the three policies (Lemmas
E.2
,
E.3
,
E.4
)

Let
S^{K}
and
S^{U}
be the sets of known and unknown products in
S
respectively. We define
W^{U}(S)\coloneq\sum_{i\in S^{U}}h(\mathcal{F}_{i})
to be the sum of the attraction parameters of the unknown entrants in
S
. For an option
i\in S^{K}\cup\{0\}
, we denote by
n_{i}(S)
the expected number of rounds that
i
is chosen during the epoch if
S
is offered repeatedly. The following lemma provides an expression for
n_{i}(S)
.

Lemma E.6
.

For any assortment
S
, and any option
i\in S^{K}\cup\{0\}
,
n_{i}(S)=\frac{w_{i}}{W^{U}(S)}
.

Proof of Lemma
E.6
.

Let
\tau(S)
be the expected number of rounds until an unknown entrant is purchased. Given that an unknown entrant is purchased with probability
\frac{W^{U}(S)}{\sum_{i\in S}w_{i}+w_{0}}
,
\tau(S)=\frac{\sum_{i\in S}w_{i}+w_{0}}{W^{U}(S)}
. Thus,

n_{i}(S)={\mathbb{P}}[\text{$i$ is chosen}|S]\tau(S)=\frac{w_{i}}{\sum_{i\in S}w_{i}+w_{0}}\frac{\sum_{i\in S}w_{i}+w_{0}}{W^{U}(S)}=\frac{w_{i}}{W^{U}(S)}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
E.2
.

An unknown product is purchased twice (once at the end of each of the two epochs), contributing
2(\textsc{OPT}-1)
regret in total. By Lemma
E.6
, the outside option is purchased
\frac{1}{h_{1}}
times in expectation in epoch one and
\frac{1}{h_{2}}
times in expectation in epoch two, contributing
\left(\frac{1}{h_{1}}+\frac{1}{h_{2}}\right)\textsc{OPT}
regret in total. By Lemma
E.6
, incumbent
3
is purchased
\frac{w_{3}}{h_{1}}
times in expectation in epoch one and the most attractive known product from
1
and
3
is purchased
\frac{\max(w_{1},w_{3})}{h_{2}}
times in expectation in epoch two, contributing
\left(\frac{w_{3}}{h_{1}}+\frac{\max(w_{1},w_{3})}{h_{2}}\right)(\textsc{OPT}-1)
regret in total. Adding the regrets and taking expectation yields the lemma. ∎

Proof of Lemma
E.3
.

The proof has the same steps as for Lemma
E.2
replacing entrant 1 with entrant 2.
∎

Proof of Lemma
E.4
.

An unknown product is purchased twice (once at the end of each of the two epochs), contributing
2(\textsc{OPT}-1)
regret in total. By Lemma
E.6
, the outside option is chosen
\frac{1}{h_{1}+h_{2}}
times in expectation in epoch one, and
\frac{1}{h_{2}}
times in expectation in epoch two if entrant 1 is revealed first (with probability
\frac{h_{1}}{h_{1}+h_{2}}
) and
\frac{1}{h_{1}}
times in expectation in epoch two if entrant 2 is revealed first (with probability
\frac{h_{2}}{h_{1}+h_{2}}
). As a result, the total regret from the outside option is
\left(\frac{1}{h_{1}+h_{2}}+\frac{1}{h_{2}}\frac{h_{1}}{h_{1}+h_{2}}+\frac{1}{h_{1}}\frac{h_{2}}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\right)\textsc{OPT}
. By Lemma
E.6
, the most attractive from products 1 and 3 is chosen
\frac{\max(w_{1},w_{3})}{h_{2}}
rounds in expectation if entrant
1
is revealed first (with probability
\frac{h_{1}}{h_{1}+h_{2}}
) and the most attractive from products 2 and 3 is chosen
\frac{\max(w_{2},w_{3})}{h_{1}}
rounds in expectation if entrant
1
is revealed first (with probability
\frac{h_{2}}{h_{1}+h_{2}}
). As a result the total regret from known products is
\left(\frac{h_{1}}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\frac{\max(w_{1},w_{3})}{h_{2}}+\frac{h_{2}}{h_{1}+h_{2}}\frac{\max(w_{2},w_{3})}{h_{1}}\right)(\textsc{OPT}-1)
. Adding the regrets, factoring out
\frac{1}{h_{1}+h_{2}}
, and taking an expectation yields the lemma. ∎

E.3
Stochastic dominance
(Proposition
6.1
)

Let
\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})
be the sum of the two largest parameters from the set
\{w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4}\}
.

Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

Given that
\mathcal{F}_{1}
stochastically dominates
\mathcal{F}_{2}
, there exists a random variable
\tilde{w}_{2}
which is coupled with
w_{1}
such that
w_{1}\geq\tilde{w}_{2}
and
\tilde{w}_{2}
is independent of
w_{2}
. By Lemma
E.1
and rearranging terms,

\displaystyle\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{2})-\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1})=\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\Bigg[\Bigg(\frac{\max(w_{2}-w_{3},0)}{h_{1}}-\frac{\max(w_{1}-w_{3},0)}{h_{2}}\Bigg)(\textsc{OPT}-1)\Bigg]

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{h_{1}}\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{\max(w_{1}-w_{3},0)-\max(w_{2}-w_{3},0)}{\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}\right]\geq\frac{1}{h_{1}}\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{w}_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{\max(\tilde{w}_{2}-w_{3},0)-\max(w_{2}-w_{3},0)}{\max_{2}(\tilde{w}_{2},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}\right]=0

which establishes the proposition. The first inequality uses Lemma
E.7
(stated and proven below). The second inequality uses that
w_{1}\geq\tilde{w}_{2}
and that the function
f(x)=\frac{(x-w_{3})^{+}-(w_{2}-w_{3})^{+}}{\max_{2}(x,w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}
is increasing in
x
by Lemma
E.8
(stated and proven below). The last equality uses that
\tilde{w}_{2}
and
w_{2}
are independent and identically distributed and that the function inside the expectation is antisymmetric.
∎

Lemma E.7
.

For any prior distributions
\mathcal{F}_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}_{2}
and
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
with
h_{1}>h_{2}
,

\underbrace{\expect\Bigg[\Bigg(\frac{\max(w_{2}-w_{3},0)}{h_{1}}-\frac{\max(w_{1}-w_{3},0)}{h_{2}}\Bigg)(\textsc{OPT}-1)\Bigg]}_{=\textsc{LHS}}>\frac{1}{h_{1}}\expect\left[\frac{\max(w_{1}-w_{3},0)-\max(w_{2}-w_{3},0)}{\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}\right]

Proof of Lemma
E.7
.

Using
\textsc{OPT}-1=-\frac{1}{\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}
, the left hand side of the lemma is

\textsc{LHS}=\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{\frac{\max(w_{1}-w_{3},0)}{h_{2}}}{\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}\right]-\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{\frac{\max(w_{2}-w_{3},0)}{h_{1}}}{\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}\right]

(46)

The first term is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{\frac{\max(w_{1}-w_{3},0)}{h_{2}}}{\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}\right]=\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{\frac{w_{1}-w_{3}}{h_{2}}}{\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}\middle|w_{1}>w_{3}\right]{\mathbb{P}}[w_{1}>w_{3}]

\displaystyle>\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{\frac{w_{1}-w_{3}}{h_{1}}}{\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}\middle|w_{1}>w_{3}\right]{\mathbb{P}}[w_{1}>w_{3}]=\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{\frac{\max(w_{1}-w_{3},0)}{h_{1}}}{\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}\right]

(47)

where the inequality uses that
\frac{w_{1}-w_{3}}{h_{2}}>\frac{w_{1}-w_{3}}{h_{1}}
conditioned on
w_{1}>w_{3}
since
h_{1}>h_{2}
and
{\mathbb{P}}[w_{1}>w_{3}]>0
. The lemma combines (
46
) and (
E.3
) and factors out
\frac{1}{h_{1}}
.
∎

.

Lemma E.8
.

For any
w_{2}
, the function
f(x)\coloneq\frac{(x-w_{3})^{+}-(w_{2}-w_{3})^{+}}{\max_{2}(x,w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}
is non-decreasing in
x
.

Proof of Lemma
E.8
.

We consider cases based on whether
w_{2}\geq w_{3}
or
w_{3}>w_{2}
.

Case 1:
w_{2}\geq w_{3}
.

Using that
w_{3}\geq w_{4}
,

f(x)=\frac{(x-w_{3})^{+}-(w_{2}-w_{3})^{+}}{\max_{2}(x,w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}=\frac{(x-w_{3})^{+}-(w_{2}-w_{3})}{\max_{2}(x,w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}=\begin{cases}\frac{x-w_{2}}{x+w_{3}+1},&x\geq w_{2},\\
\frac{w_{3}-w_{2}}{w_{3}+w_{2}+1},&x<w_{2}.\end{cases}

Thus,
f(x)=\max\left(\frac{x-w_{2}}{x+w_{3}+1},\frac{w_{3}-w_{2}}{w_{3}+w_{3}+1}\right)
.
f(x)
is non-decreasing as
\frac{x-w_{2}}{x+w_{3}+1}
is increasing and the maximum of an increasing function and a constant is non-decreasing.

Case 2:
w_{3}>w_{2}
.

Using that
w_{3}\geq w_{4}
,

f(x)=\frac{(x-w_{3})^{+}-(w_{2}-w_{3})^{+}}{\max_{2}(x,w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}=\frac{(x-w_{3})^{+}}{\max_{2}(x,w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}=\begin{cases}\frac{x-w_{3}}{x+w_{3}+1},&x\geq w_{3},\\
0,&x<w_{3}.\end{cases}

Thus,
f(x)=\max\left(\frac{x-w_{3}}{x+w_{3}+1},0\right)
.
f(x)
is non-decreasing as
\frac{x-w_{3}}{x+w_{3}+1}
is increasing and the maximum of an increasing function and a constant is non-decreasing.
∎

E.4
Optimal policy under two Bernoulli-like priors (Proposition
6.2
)

Let
\mathcal{I}(\mu,p_{1},p_{2},w_{3},w_{4})
be the instance where the attraction parameter of entrant
i\in\{1,2\}
equals
\frac{\mu}{p_{i}}
with probability
p_{i}
and
0
otherwise, the incumbents have attraction parameters
w_{3}
and
w_{4}
with
w_{3}>w_{4}
, and
h
is the mean. To prove Proposition
6.2
, the following lemma (proof in Appendix
E.5
) shows that when the prior upside probabilities
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are smaller than
\frac{1}{6}
, exploring both entrants is not optimal.

Lemma E.9
.

For any
\mu,p_{1},p_{2},w_{3},w_{4},
with
p_{1},p_{2}\in(0,\frac{1}{6})
,
w_{3}<\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}
,
w_{3}<\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}
,
w_{3}>2\mu
,
w_{3}>4
and
w_{4}=\frac{w_{3}}{2}
,
\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1})<\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1,2})
under the instance
\mathcal{I}(\mu,p_{1},p_{2},w_{3},w_{4})
.

The next lemma (proof in Appendix
E.6
) shows that when the most attractive incumbent’s attraction parameter lies within a moderate range, either
\pi^{1}
or
\pi^{2}
can yield lower regret and the behavior depends on a threshold
\theta
.

Lemma E.10
.

For any
\mu,p_{1},w_{3},w_{4}
with
w_{3}<\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}<2w_{3}
,
w_{3}>2\mu
, there exists some
\theta(\mu,p_{1},w_{3})\in(0,p_{1})
such that
\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{2})<\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1})
if
p_{2}\in(p_{1}-\theta(\mu,p_{1},w_{3}),p_{1})
and
\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{2})>\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1})
if
p_{2}\in(0,p_{1}-\theta(\mu,p_{1},w_{3}))
under the instance
\mathcal{I}(\mu,p_{1},p_{2},w_{3},w_{4})
.

The next lemma (proof in Appendix
E.7
) shows that there exists an optimal policy which offers the same assortment until an unknown entrant is purchased.

Lemma E.11
.

There exists an optimal policy
\pi^{\star}
such that
\pi^{\star}\in\{\pi^{1},\pi^{2},\pi^{1,2}\}
.

Proof of Proposition
6.2
.

Any parameters
\mu,p_{1},p_{2},w_{3},w_{4}
with
p_{2}<p_{1}
,
p_{1}\in(0,\frac{1}{6})
,
w_{3}<\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}<2w_{3}
,
w_{3}>2\mu
,
w_{3}>4
, and
w_{4}=\frac{w_{3}}{2}
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
E.9
and Lemma
E.10
. By Lemma
E.9
,
\pi^{1,2}
is not optimal. Combining this with Lemma
E.10
and Lemma
E.11
,
\pi^{1}
is optimal if
p_{2}<\theta(\mu,p_{1},w_{3})
and
\pi^{2}
is optimal if
p_{2}>\theta(\mu,p_{1},w_{3})
.
∎

E.5
Exploring both entrants is not optimal (Lemma
E.9
)

Proof of Lemma
E.9
.

Letting
\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})
be the sum of the two largest parameters from the set
\{w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4}\}
, the scaled difference of the regrets of
\pi^{1,2}
and
\pi^{1}
is expressed and lower bounded as

\displaystyle 2\mu\left(\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1,2})-\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1})\right)

\displaystyle=\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\Bigg[\frac{-\max(w_{2},w_{3})+\max(w_{1},w_{3})+2w_{3}-\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})}{\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=p_{1}p_{2}\frac{-\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}+\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}+2w_{3}-(\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}+\frac{\mu}{p_{2}})}{\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}+\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}+1}+p_{1}(1-p_{2})\frac{-w_{3}+\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}+2w_{3}-(\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}+w_{3})}{\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}+w_{3}+1}

\displaystyle+(1-p_{1})p_{2}\frac{-\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}+w_{3}+2w_{3}-(\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}+w_{3})}{\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}+w_{3}+1}+(1-p_{1})(1-p_{2})\frac{w_{3}-w_{4}}{w_{3}+w_{4}+1}

\displaystyle=p_{1}p_{2}\frac{2(w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{2}})}{\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}+\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}+1}+p_{2}(1-p_{1})\frac{2(w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{2}})}{\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}+w_{3}+1}+(1-p_{1})(1-p_{2})\frac{w_{3}}{3w_{3}+2}>0.

which shows the lemma (as
\mu>0
). The first equality expresses the regret difference between
\pi^{1,2}
and
\pi^{1}
using Lemma
E.1
, uses
h_{1}=h_{2}=\mu
(as
h
is the mean),
\textsc{OPT}=\frac{\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})}{\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}
, and
\textsc{OPT}-1=-\frac{1}{\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}
and cancels the
\mu
term. The second equality expands the expectation over
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
and uses
\max(\frac{\mu}{p_{1}},w_{3})=\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}
(as
\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}>w_{3}
) and
\max(\frac{\mu}{p_{2}},w_{3})=\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}
(as
\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}>w_{3}
). The third equality cancels the term in multiplying
p_{1}(1-p_{2})
as
-w_{3}+\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}+2w_{3}-(\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}+w_{3})=0
and uses
\frac{w_{3}-w_{4}}{w_{3}+w_{4}+1}=\frac{w_{3}}{3w_{3}+2}
as
w_{4}=\frac{w_{3}}{2}
. The inequality uses Lemma
E.12
(stated and proven below). ∎

Lemma E.12
.

For any
\mu,p_{1},p_{2},w_{3},w_{4},
with
p_{1},p_{2}\in(0,\frac{1}{6})
,
w_{3}<\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}
,
w_{3}<\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}
,
w_{3}>2\mu
,
w_{3}>4
,

\underbrace{p_{1}p_{2}\frac{2(w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{2}})}{\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}+\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}+1}+p_{2}(1-p_{1})\frac{2(w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{2}})}{\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}+w_{3}+1}+(1-p_{1})(1-p_{2})\frac{w_{3}}{3w_{3}+2}}_{\eqqcolon\textsc{LHS}}>0

Proof.

The left hand side is lower bounded by

LHS

\displaystyle\geq p_{2}\frac{2(w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{2}})}{\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}+w_{3}+1}+(1-p_{1})(1-p_{2})\frac{w_{3}}{3w_{3}+2}\geq\frac{2p_{2}(w_{3}-\frac{w_{3}}{2p_{2}})}{\frac{w_{3}}{2p_{2}}+w_{3}+1}+(1-p_{1})(1-p_{2})\frac{w_{3}}{3w_{3}+2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2p_{2}-1)w_{3}}{\frac{w_{3}}{2p_{2}}+w_{3}+1}+(1-p_{2})(1-p_{1})\frac{w_{3}}{3w_{3}+2}\geq\frac{(p_{2}-1)w_{3}}{4w_{3}+1}+(1-p_{2})\frac{5w_{3}}{18w_{3}+12}

\displaystyle=(1-p_{2})\left(\frac{w_{3}}{4w_{3}+1}-\frac{5w_{3}}{18w_{3}+12}\right)=(1-p_{2})\frac{w_{3}(2w_{3}-7)}{(4w_{3}+1)(18w_{3}+12)}>0.

The first inequality uses
\frac{2(w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{2}})}{\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}+\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}+1}\geq\frac{2(w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{2}})}{\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}+w_{3}+1}
as
\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}\geq w_{3}
and
w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}\leq 0
to lower bound the first term of the
LHS
and expands the second term to cancel the terms multiplied by
p_{1}p_{2}
. The second inequality uses
p_{2}\frac{2(w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{2}})}{\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}+w_{3}+1}\geq\frac{2p_{2}(w_{3}-\frac{w_{3}}{2p_{2}})}{\frac{w_{3}}{2p_{2}}+w_{3}+1}
since
\frac{w_{3}}{2}\geq\mu
and the function
f(x)\coloneq\frac{w_{3}-\frac{x}{p_{2}}}{\frac{x}{p_{2}}+w_{3}+1}
is decreasing in
x
. The third inequality uses that
2p_{2}-1\geq p_{2}-1
,
\frac{w_{3}}{2p_{2}}+w_{3}\geq 4w_{3}
as
p_{2}<\frac{1}{6}
, and
(1-p_{1})\frac{w_{3}}{3w_{3}+2}\geq\frac{5w_{3}}{18w_{3}+12}
since
1-p_{1}>\frac{5}{6}
(as
p_{1}<\frac{1}{6}
). The last inequality uses
2w_{3}-7>0
as
w_{3}>4
.
∎

E.6
Threshold dependence of optimal exploration strategy (Lemma
E.10
)

Lemma E.13
.

There exists a quadratic
Q_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}(x)=x^{2}\cdot A_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}+x\cdot B_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}+C_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}
such that

1.

\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{2})>\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1})
if and only if
Q_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}(p_{2})<0
,

2.

A_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}>0
and the quadratic
Q_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}(\cdot)
has one positive root and one negative root, and

3.

evaluated at
x=p_{1}
,
Q_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}
is positive, i.e.,
Q_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}(p_{1})>0
.

Proof of Lemma
E.10
.

Let
Q_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}(x)
be the quadratic in Lemma
E.13
and
\theta(\mu,p_{1},w_{3})
be its positive root. Given that
A_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}>0
,
Q_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}(p_{1})
has one positive and one negative root and
Q_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}(p_{1})>0
(by Lemma
E.13
), then
\theta(\mu,p_{1},w_{3})\in(0,p_{1})
.

By Lemma
E.13
,
\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{2})>\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1})
when
p_{2}\in(0,\theta(\mu,p_{1},w_{3}))
because
Q_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}(p_{2})<0
, and
\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{2})<\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1})
when
p_{2}\in(\theta(\mu,p_{1},w_{3}),p_{1})
because
Q_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}(p_{2})>0
completing the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
E.13
.

Letting
\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})
be the sum of two largest attraction parameters from
\{w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4}\}
, the scaled difference between the regrets of
\pi^{2}
and
\pi^{1}
is

\displaystyle\mu\left(\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{2})-\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1})\right)=\expect_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}\sim\mathcal{F}_{1}\\
w_{2}\sim\mathcal{F}_{2}\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{\max(w_{1},w_{3})-\max(w_{2},w_{3})}{\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}\right]

\displaystyle=(p_{2}-p_{1})\frac{p_{2}\cdot\mu p_{1}}{p_{2}\cdot(\mu+p_{1})+\mu p_{1}}+\frac{p_{2}(1-p_{1})(p_{2}w_{3}-\mu)}{p_{2}(w_{3}+1)+\mu}-\frac{p_{1}(1-p_{2})(p_{1}w_{3}-\mu)}{p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu}

\displaystyle=(p_{2}-p_{1})\frac{p_{2}\cdot\mu p_{1}}{p_{2}\cdot(\mu+p_{1})+\mu p_{1}}+\frac{(p_{2}-p_{1})\Bigg(p_{2}\cdot\Big(p_{1}\big(w_{3}(w_{3}-2\mu)+w_{3}-\mu\big)+w_{3}\mu\Big)+\mu p_{1}\left(w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}\right)\Bigg)}{p_{2}\cdot(w_{3}+1)(p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)+\mu(p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)}

\displaystyle=(p_{2}-p_{1})\cdot\frac{p_{2}^{2}\cdot A_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}+p_{2}\cdot B_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}+C_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}}{(p_{2}\cdot(\mu+p_{1})+\mu p_{1})(p_{2}\cdot(w_{3}+1)(p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)+\mu(p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu))}

(48)

\displaystyle\text{for }A_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\mu p_{1}(w_{3}+1)(p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)+\Big(p_{1}\big(w_{3}(w_{3}-2\mu)+w_{3}-\mu\big)+w_{3}\mu\Big)(\mu+p_{1}),

\displaystyle B_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\mu^{2}p_{1}(p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)+\mu p_{1}\left(w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}\right)(\mu+p_{1})+\mu p_{1}\Big(p_{1}\big(w_{3}(w_{3}-2\mu)+w_{3}-\mu\big)+w_{3}\mu\Big),

\displaystyle C_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\mu^{2}p_{1}^{2}\left(w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}\right).

The first equality in (
E.6
) expresses the regret difference between
\pi^{2}
and
\pi^{1}
using Lemma
E.1
, uses
h_{1}=h_{2}=\mu
(as
h
is the mean),
\textsc{OPT}=\frac{\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})}{\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}
, and
\textsc{OPT}-1=-\frac{1}{\max_{2}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})+1}
and cancels the
\mu
term. The second equality expands the expectation over
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
, using
\max(\frac{\mu}{p_{1}},w_{3})=\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}
(as
\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}>w_{3}
) and
\max(\frac{\mu}{p_{2}},w_{3})=\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}
(as
\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}>w_{3}
), and performs an algebraic manipulation on each fraction. The third equality uses Lemma
E.14
(stated and proven below). The last equality factors out
p_{2}-p_{1}
and takes a common denominator.

Let
Q_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}(c)\coloneq x^{2}\cdot A_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}+x\cdot B_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}+C_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}
. Given that the numerator of the right hand side of (
E.6
) is
Q_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}(p_{2})
, the denominator is positive and
p_{2}-p_{1}<0
,
\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{2})>\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{1})
if and only if
Q_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}(p_{2})<0
showing the first lemma part. The second-degree term of
Q
,
A_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}
, is positive

A_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}>\Big(p_{1}\big(w_{3}(w_{3}-2\mu)+w_{3}-\mu\big)+w_{3}\mu\Big)(\mu+p_{1})\geq 0

where the first inequality uses that
\mu p_{1}(w_{3}+1)(p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)>0
as
\mu,p_{1},w_{3}>0
and the second inequality uses that
w_{3}>2\mu
and
w_{3}>\mu
. The constant term of
Q
,
C_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}
, is negative
C_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}=\mu^{2}p_{1}^{2}\left(w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}\right)<0
as
\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}>w_{3}
. As a result,
Q_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}(x)
has one positive and one negative root showing the second lemma part. The value of
Q_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}
at
p_{1}
is lower bounded by

\displaystyle Q_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}(p_{1})

\displaystyle=p_{1}^{2}\cdot A_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}+p_{1}\cdot B_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}+C_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}

\displaystyle\geq p_{1}^{2}\Big(p_{1}\big(w_{3}(w_{3}-2\mu)+w_{3}-\mu\big)+w_{3}\mu\Big)(\mu+p_{1})

\displaystyle+p_{1}\left(\mu p_{1}\left(w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}\right)(\mu+p_{1})+\mu p_{1}\Big(p_{1}\big(w_{3}(w_{3}-2\mu)+w_{3}-\mu\big)+w_{3}\mu\Big)\right)+\mu^{2}p_{1}^{2}\left(w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(p_{1}\Big(p_{1}\big(w_{3}(w_{3}-2\mu)+w_{3}-\mu\big)+w_{3}\mu\Big)+\mu p_{1}\left(w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}\right)\right)\left(p_{1}(\mu+p_{1})+\mu p_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(p_{1}^{2}\big(w_{3}(w_{3}-2\mu)+w_{3}-\mu\big)+\mu p_{1}\left(2w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}\right)\right)\left(p_{1}(\mu+p_{1})+\mu p_{1}\right)>0

showing the third lemma part and completing the proof. The first inequality uses
\mu p_{1}(w_{3}+1)(p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)>0
to lower bound
A_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}
and
\mu^{2}p_{1}(p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)>0
to lower bound
B_{\mu,p_{1},w_{3}}
. The last inequality uses
w_{3}>2\mu
,
w_{3}>\mu
, and
2w_{3}>\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}
.
∎

Lemma E.14
.

For any
\mu,p_{1},p_{1}
and
w_{3}
,

\underbrace{\frac{p_{2}(1-p_{1})(p_{2}w_{3}-\mu)}{p_{2}(w_{3}+1)+\mu}-\frac{p_{1}(1-p_{2})(p_{1}w_{3}-\mu)}{p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu}}_{\eqqcolon\textsc{LHS}}=\frac{(p_{2}-p_{1})\left(p_{2}\cdot D_{1}(\mu,p_{1},w_{3})+D_{0}(\mu,p_{1},w_{3})\right)}{p_{2}\cdot(w_{3}+1)(p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)+\mu(p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)}

where
D_{1}(\mu,p_{1},w_{3})=p_{1}\big(w_{3}(w_{3}-2\mu)+w_{3}-\mu\big)+w_{3}\mu
and
D_{0}(\mu,p_{1},w_{3})=\mu p_{1}(w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{1}})
.

Proof of Lemma
E.14
.

Taking a common denominator in the left hand side,

LHS

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{2}(1-p_{1})(p_{2}w_{3}-\mu)(p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)-p_{1}(1-p_{2})(p_{1}w_{3}-\mu)(p_{2}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)}{p_{2}\cdot(w_{3}+1)(p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)+\mu(p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)}

\displaystyle=(p_{2}-p_{1})\frac{p_{2}\cdot D_{1}(\mu,p_{1},w_{3})+D_{0}(\mu,p_{1},w_{3})}{p_{2}\cdot(w_{3}+1)(p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)+\mu(p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)}.

To obtain the second equality, express the terms in the numerator as

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\coloneq p_{2}(1-p_{1})(p_{2}w_{3}-\mu)(p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)=(p_{2}^{2}w_{3}-p_{2}\mu-p_{1}p_{2}^{2}w_{3}+p_{2}p_{1}\mu)(p_{1}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)

\displaystyle=p_{1}p_{2}^{2}w_{3}(w_{3}+1)+\mu p_{2}^{2}w_{3}-p_{1}p_{2}\mu(w_{3}+1)-p_{2}\mu^{2}-p_{1}^{2}p_{2}^{2}w_{3}(w_{3}+1)-\mu p_{1}p_{2}^{2}w_{3}+p_{1}^{2}p_{2}\mu(w_{3}+1)

\displaystyle+p_{2}p_{1}\mu^{2},\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\coloneq p_{1}(1-p_{2})(p_{1}w_{3}-\mu)(p_{2}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)=(p_{1}^{2}w_{3}-p_{1}\mu-p_{2}p_{1}^{2}w_{3}+p_{1}p_{2}\mu)(p_{2}(w_{3}+1)+\mu)

\displaystyle=p_{2}p_{1}^{2}w_{3}(w_{3}+1)+\mu p_{1}^{2}w_{3}-p_{2}p_{1}\mu(w_{3}+1)-p_{1}\mu^{2}-p_{2}^{2}p_{1}^{2}w_{3}(w_{3}+1)-\mu p_{2}p_{1}^{2}w_{3}+p_{2}^{2}p_{1}\mu(w_{3}+1)

\displaystyle+p_{1}p_{2}\mu^{2},

Canceling the common terms
p_{2}p_{1}\mu(w_{3}+1)
,
p_{2}^{2}p_{1}^{2}w_{3}(w_{3}+1)
, and
p_{1}p_{2}\mu^{2}
, the numerator is

\displaystyle X-Y

\displaystyle=w_{3}(w_{3}+1)p_{1}p_{2}(p_{2}-p_{1})+\mu w_{3}(p_{2}-p_{1})(p_{2}+p_{1})-\mu^{2}(p_{2}-p_{1})-\mu p_{1}p_{2}w_{3}(p_{2}-p_{1})

\displaystyle+p_{1}p_{2}\mu(w_{3}+1)(p_{1}-p_{2})

\displaystyle=(p_{2}-p_{1})\Bigg(p_{2}\Big(p_{1}\big(w_{3}(w_{3}-2\mu)+w_{3}-\mu\big)+w_{3}\mu\Big)+\mu p_{1}\left(w_{3}-\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}\right)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=(p_{2}-p_{1})(p_{2}\cdot D_{1}(\mu,p_{1},w_{3})+D_{0}(\mu,p_{1},w_{3}))

∎

E.7
Optimality of one of the three policies (Lemma
E.11
)

To prove Lemma
E.11
we first characterize the optimal policy through a set of optimal regret functions in Lemma
E.15
, which is similar to Lemma
4.1
. To do so, for a history
\mathcal{H}=\{(S_{i},Y_{i},w_{Y_{i}})\}_{i=1}^{t-1}
, we define
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})=\{(i,w_{i}):i\in I(\mathcal{H})\}
to be the
condensed history
consisting of the known products and their respective attraction parameters given
\mathcal{H}
. The set of known products and the attraction parameter of each product given a history
\mathcal{H}
depend on
\mathcal{H}
only through the condensed history
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})
. As a result, the expected ex-post optimum,
\textsc{OPT}(\mathcal{H})
, the revenue of a set
S
given
\mathcal{H}
,
\textsc{Rev}(S;\mathcal{H})
, and the epoch regret of a set
S
given
\mathcal{H}
,
\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{H})
, depend on
\mathcal{H}
only through
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})
and we let

\textsc{OPT}(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))\coloneq\textsc{OPT}(\mathcal{H})\text{,}\quad\textsc{Rev}(S,\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))\coloneq\textsc{Rev}(S;\mathcal{H})\text{,}\quad\textsc{EpochReg}(S,\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))\coloneq\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{H}).

The sets of known products
I(\mathcal{H})
and assortments containing an unknown entrant
\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})
depend on
\mathcal{H}
only through the condensed history; as a result
I(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))\coloneq I(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))\coloneq\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})
.

We define optimal regret functions
V(\mathcal{C})
and
Q(S;\mathcal{C})
for any condensed history
\mathcal{C}
and assortment
S\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{C})
containing an unknown entrant, with a recursive definition based on the number of unknown entrants remaining in
\mathcal{C}
. If there are no unknown entrants given
\mathcal{C}
, set
V(\mathcal{C})\coloneqq 0
. Suppose we have defined
V(\mathcal{C})
and
Q(S;\mathcal{C})
for any condensed history
\mathcal{C}
with at most
k
remaining unknown entrants and
S\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{C})
. A condensed history
\mathcal{C}
is
terminal
if
\textsc{OPT}(\mathcal{C})=\max\limits_{S\subseteq I(\mathcal{C}),|S|\leq c}\textsc{Rev}(S,\mathcal{C})
. For any terminal
\mathcal{C}
with
k+1
unknown entrants set
Q(S,\mathcal{C})\coloneq 0
for all
S\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{C})
and
V(\mathcal{C})\coloneq 0
. Let
S^{\textsc{u}}(\mathcal{C})
be the subset of unknown entrants in
S
given a condensed history
\mathcal{C}
. For any non-terminal condensed history
\mathcal{C}
with
k+1
unknown entrants and
S\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{C})
,

Q(S;\mathcal{C})\coloneqq\textsc{EpochReg}(S,\mathcal{C})+\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}(\mathcal{C})}\frac{h(\mathcal{F}_{i})}{\sum_{j\in S^{\textsc{u}}(\mathcal{C})}h(\mathcal{F}_{j})}\expect_{w_{i}\sim\mathcal{F}_{i}}[V(\mathcal{C}\cup(i,w_{i}))]\quad\text{and}\quad V(\mathcal{C})\coloneqq\min_{S\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{C})}Q(S,\mathcal{C}).

The following lemma (proven in Appendix
E.8
) establishes that a policy is optimal if it selects the assortment
S\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{C})
minimizing
Q(S;\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))
if
\mathcal{H}
is not terminal and the revenue-maximizing assortment otherwise. This lemma is the analogue of Lemma
4.1
in this heterogeneous priors’ model and the optimal regret function
Q(S;\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))
has a similar role as the epoch regret
\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{H})
in Lemma
4.1
.

Lemma E.15
.

A policy
\pi^{\star}
is optimal if for any history
\mathcal{H}
,

•

\pi^{\star}(\mathcal{H})\in\argmin\limits_{S\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})}Q(S;\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))

if
\mathcal{H}
is not terminal, and

•

\pi^{\star}(\mathcal{H})\in\argmax\limits_{S\subseteq I(\mathcal{H}),|S|\leq c}\textsc{Rev}(S;\mathcal{H})
otherwise.

Proof of Lemma
E.11
.

Any policy which (i) offers the same assortment until an unknown entrant is purchased, (ii) always explores at least one unknown entrant until all entrants are purchased, and (iii) offers unknown entrants with the most attractive known products is
\pi^{1}
,
\pi^{2}
, or
\pi^{1,2}
. Let
\pi^{\star}
be a policy which satisfies the conditions of Lemma
E.15
and satisfies (i), i.e., it offers the same assortment until an unknown entrant is purchased. To prove the lemma it suffices to show that
\pi^{\star}
explores at least one unknown entrant until all entrants have been purchased and that
\pi^{\star}
always complements the unknown entrants with the most attractive known products.

(ii)
\pi^{\star}
explores at least one unknown entrant until all entrants have been purchased.

Given that the upsides of both entrants
\frac{\mu}{p_{1}}
and
\frac{\mu}{p_{2}}
are greater than the the most attractive incumbent’s attraction parameter
w_{3}
, a history is terminal if and only if both entrants have been purchased. Thus,
\pi^{\star}
explores at least one entrant until both entrants have been purchased.

(iii)
\pi^{\star}
explores the unknown entrants with the most attractive known products.

For any history
\mathcal{H}
,
\pi^{\star}(\mathcal{H})\in\argmin\limits_{S\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})}Q(S;\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))
. Let
S^{K}
and
S^{U}
be the known and unknown products in
\pi^{\star}(\mathcal{H})
respectively.
Given that the to-go regret does not depend on
S^{K}
, the minimization problem reduces to minimizing
\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))
. When a known product
i\in S^{K}
is purchased a regret of
\textsc{OPT}-1
is incurred; this occurs
\frac{w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}{\sum_{i\in S^{U}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}
rounds in expectation. When the outside option is chosen a regret of
OPT
is incurred; this occurs
\frac{w_{0}}{\sum_{i\in S^{U}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}
rounds in expectation. When an unknown entrant is purchased a regret of
\textsc{OPT}-1
is incurred; this happens once. As result, the epoch regret is

\textsc{EpochReg}(S,\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))=(\textsc{OPT}-1)\frac{\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}{\sum_{i\in S^{U}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}+\textsc{OPT}\frac{w_{0}}{\sum_{i\in S^{U}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}+(\textsc{OPT}-1).

Thus minimizing
\textsc{EpochReg}(S,\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))
conditioned on
S^{U}
is equivalent to maximizing the sum of the known products’ attraction parameters
\sum_{i\in S^{K}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})
, i.e., selecting the most attractive
c-|S^{U}|
known products.
∎

E.8
Optimality of stationary and deterministic policies (Lemma
E.15
)

To prove Lemma
E.15
we need several auxiliary lemmas similar to the proof of Lemma
4.1
. Lemma
B.1
continues to hold for the policy
\pi^{\star}
in Lemma
E.15
and its proof is the same. The following lemma (proven in Appendix
E.9
) establishes that the regret of
\pi^{\star}
can be expressed through the optimal regret function
V
. This is analogous to Lemma
B.2
as the regret depends on
\mathcal{H}
through
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})
and to Lemma
B.3
as
V(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))
corresponds to a one-epoch-lookahead decomposition.

Lemma E.16
.

For any history
\mathcal{H}
,
\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H})=V(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))
.

The following lemma (proven in Appendix
E.10
) establishes that the regret of any policy
\pi
given a history
\mathcal{H}
is lower bounded by
V(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))
and is the analogue of Lemma
B.4
. Note that this is stronger than Lemma
B.4
since it establishes the inequality in both cases. This is why an analogue of Lemma
B.5
is not needed. A lemma analogous to Lemma
B.5
is used in the proof of Lemma
E.17
.

Lemma E.17
.

For any policy
\pi
and non-terminal history
\mathcal{H}
,
\textsc{Reg}(\pi;\mathcal{H})\geq V(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))
.

Proof of Lemma
E.15
.

For any policy
\pi
it suffices to show
\textsc{Rev}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H})\leq\textsc{Rev}(\pi;\mathcal{H})
for any history
\mathcal{H}
. If
\mathcal{H}
is terminal, then Lemma
B.1
implies that
\textsc{Rev}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H})=0
and
\textsc{Rev}(\pi;\mathcal{H})\geq 0
. If
\mathcal{H}
is non-terminal, then

\textsc{Rev}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H})=V(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))\leq\textsc{Rev}(\pi;\mathcal{H})
; the equality uses Lemma
E.16
and the inequality uses Lemma
E.17
.
∎

E.9
Optimal policy regret depends only on the condensed history (Lemma
E.16
).

Proof of Lemma
E.16
.

We prove that
\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H})=V(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))
by induction on the number of unknown entrants remaining in
\mathcal{H}
.

Base Case:
\mathcal{H}
contains no unknown entrants.

Then

\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H})=0
by Lemma
B.1
as
\mathcal{H}
is terminal and
V(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))=0
by the definition of
V
.

Induction Hypothesis:

\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H}^{\prime})=V(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime}))
for any history
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
with at most
k
unknown entrants.

Induction Step:

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a history with
k+1
unknown entrants. If
\mathcal{H}
is terminal, then
\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H})=0
by Lemma
B.1
and
V(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))=0
by the definition of
V
. Suppose
\mathcal{H}
is not terminal. Let
Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}
be the first round that an unknown entrant is purchased. Given that that
\pi^{\star}
offers an unknown entrant at every round until
Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}
and an unknown entrant is purchased with probability at least
\frac{\min_{i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}}h(\mathcal{F}_{i})}{(c+1)w_{\textsc{max}}}
where
w_{\textsc{max}}=\max\{w_{i}(\mathcal{H}):i\in\mathcal{N}\cup\{0\}\}
, then
\expect[Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}]\leq\frac{(c+1)w_{\textsc{max}}}{\min_{i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}}h(\mathcal{F}_{i})}
. Thus,
{\mathbb{P}}[Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}<\infty]=1
. As a result, the regret of
\pi^{\star}
is expressed as

\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H})=\underbrace{\expect\left[\sum_{t=1}^{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}}\textsc{OPT}(\mathcal{H}_{t})-\textsc{Rev}(S_{t};\mathcal{H}_{t})\middle|\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{H}\right]}_{=\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}}+\underbrace{\expect\left[\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H}_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}}\cup(S_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}},y_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}},w_{Y_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}}}))\middle|\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{H}\right]}_{=\textsc{FutureReg}^{\star}}

To express the first term, let
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{H})=\argmin\limits_{S\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})}Q(S;\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))
be the set of assortments minimizing the optimal regret function,
q(S;\mathcal{H})=\frac{\sum_{i\in S\setminus I(\mathcal{H})}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}{\sum_{i\in S}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})+w_{0}}
be the probability that an unknown entrant is purchased given
S
, and
N(S)
be the number of rounds
S
is offered until
Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}
. Similar to equation (
B.6
) in the proof of Lemma
B.9
(Appendix
B.6
), the regret until
Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{H})}\textsc{EpochReg}(S,\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))\underbrace{q(S;\mathcal{H})\expect[N(S)|\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{H}]}_{=p(S;\mathcal{H})}

The quantity
p(S;\mathcal{H})=q(S;\mathcal{H})\expect[N(S)|\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{H}]
is the expected number of times an unknown entrant is purchased following an offering from
S
until
Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}
as
S
is offered
\expect[N(S)|\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{H}]
rounds in expectation and every time
S
is offered, an unknown entrant is purchased independently with probability
q(S;\mathcal{H})
. Given that an unknown entrant is purchased only once during the epoch,
p(S;\mathcal{H})
is the probability that an unknown entrant is purchased following an offering from
S
during the epoch. Thus, the future regret is expressed as

\displaystyle\textsc{FutureReg}^{\star}

\displaystyle=\expect[V(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})\cup(Y_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}},w_{Y_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}}}))|\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{H}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{H})}p(S;\mathcal{H})\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))}\frac{h(\mathcal{F}_{i})}{\sum_{j\in S^{\textsc{u}}(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})))}h(\mathcal{F}_{j})}\expect_{w_{i}\sim\mathcal{F}_{i}}[V(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})\cup(i,w_{i}))].

The first equality uses the induction hypothesis on
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\mathcal{H}_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}}\cup(S_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}},y_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}},w_{Y_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}}})
, noting that after the first purchase of the unknown entrant at round
Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}
the condensed history is augmented by
(Y_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}},w_{Y_{Z^{\mathcal{H}}_{\star}}})
. The second equality decomposes expected future regret over the chosen assortment
S\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{H})
and the purchased entrant
i\in S^{\textsc{u}}(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))
. Therefore, the regret of
\pi^{\star}
is expressed as

\displaystyle\textsc{Reg}(\pi^{\star};\mathcal{H})=\textsc{EpochReg}^{\star}+\textsc{FutureReg}^{\star}

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{H})}p(S;\mathcal{H})\Bigg[\textsc{EpochReg}(S,\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))+\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))}\frac{h(\mathcal{F}_{i})}{\sum_{j\in S^{\textsc{u}}(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})))}h(\mathcal{F}_{j})}\expect_{w_{i}\sim\mathcal{F}_{i}}[V(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})\cup(i,w_{i}))]\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{H})}p(S;\mathcal{H})Q(S;\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))=\left[\sum_{S\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{H})}p(S;\mathcal{H})\right]\min_{S\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))}Q(S;\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))=\min_{S\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))}Q(S;\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))=V(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})).

∎

E.10
Regret of any policy is lower bounded by the value function (Lemma
E.17
)

To prove Lemma
E.17
, the following lemma lower and upper bounds the optimal regret function
V(\mathcal{C})
by constants independent of
\mathcal{C}
and is the analogue of Lemma
B.5
. Given that entrants have heterogeneous priors, instead of the reciprocal of the customer prior attraction parameter
h(\mathcal{F})
the upper bound uses the reciprocal of the minimum customer prior attraction parameter
\underline{h}=\min_{i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}}h(\mathcal{F}_{i})
.

Lemma E.18
.

For any condensed history
\mathcal{C}
,
V(\mathcal{C})\geq-m
and
V(\mathcal{C})\leq\frac{m}{\underline{h}}
.

Proof of Lemma
E.18
.

We show by induction on
k
that for any history
\mathcal{H}
with
k
unknown entrants remaining,

V(\mathcal{C})\geq-k\quad\text{ and }\quad V(\mathcal{C})\leq\frac{k}{\overline{h}}

(49)

For the base case
k=0
,
V(\mathcal{C})=0
by definition. For the induction step, suppose (
49
) holds for
k
. If
\mathcal{C}
is terminal, then
V(\mathcal{C})=0
which satisfies the conditions. Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is not terminal. Then by definition

Q(S;\mathcal{C})=\textsc{EpochReg}(S,\mathcal{C})+\underbrace{\sum_{i\in S^{\textsc{u}}(\mathcal{C})}\frac{h(\mathcal{F}_{i})}{\sum_{j\in S^{\textsc{u}}(\mathcal{C})}h(\mathcal{F}_{j})}\expect_{w_{i}\sim\mathcal{F}_{i}}[V(\mathcal{C}\cup(i,w_{i}))]}_{=(\star)}.

By the induction hypothesis (
49
) ,
(\star)\geq-k
and
(\star)\leq\frac{k}{\overline{h}}
. Given that
V(\mathcal{C})=\min_{S\in\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{H})}Q(S;\mathcal{C})
, to finish the proof it suffices to show
\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{C})\geq-1
and
\textsc{EpochReg}(S;\mathcal{C})\leq\frac{1}{\overline{h}}
. The former inequality is shown in the same way as (
B.9
) in Lemma
B.5
(Appendix
B.9
). The latter is shown in the same way as (
B.9
) in Lemma
B.5
(Appendix
B.9
) where the last inequality is replaced with
\frac{1}{\sum_{i\in S^{U}}w_{i}(\mathcal{H})}\leq\frac{1}{\overline{h}}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
E.17
. .

We proceed by induction on the number of unknown entrants in
\mathcal{H}
.

Base case.

If
\mathcal{H}
has no unknown entrants,
\textsc{Reg}(\pi;\mathcal{H})\geq 0
by Lemma
B.1
and
V(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))=0
by definition.

Induction Hypothesis.

\textsc{Reg}(\pi;\mathcal{H}^{\prime})\geq V(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime}))
for any history
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
with at most
k
unknown entrants.

Induction Step.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a history with
k+1
unknown entrants. If
\mathcal{H}
is terminal, then
\textsc{Reg}(\pi;\mathcal{H})\geq 0
by Lemma
B.1
and
V(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}))=0
by definition. If
\mathcal{H}
is not terminal, let
Z
be the round at which the first unknown entrant is purchased and
N(S)
be the number of rounds
S
is offered until
Z
. Then

\displaystyle\textsc{Reg}(\pi;\mathcal{H})=\expect\left[\sum_{t=1}^{Z}\textsc{OPT}(\mathcal{H}_{t})-\textsc{Rev}(S_{t};\mathcal{H}_{t})\middle|\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{H}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 46.97505pt+\expect\left[\textsc{Reg}(\pi;\mathcal{H}_{Z}\cup(S_{Z},Y_{Z},w_{Y_{Z}}))\middle|\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{H},Z<\infty\right]{\mathbb{P}}[Z<\infty]

\displaystyle\geq\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq\mathcal{N},|S|\leq c\end{subarray}}(\textsc{OPT}(\mathcal{H})-\textsc{Rev}(S;\mathcal{H}))\expect[N(S)|\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{H}]}_{=\textsc{EpochReg}^{\pi}(\mathcal{H})}+\underbrace{\expect[V(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})\cup(Y_{Z},w_{Y_{Z}}))|\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{H}]}_{=\textsc{FutureReg}^{\pi}(\mathcal{H})}{\mathbb{P}}[Z<\infty]

(50)

The first term of the inequality expresses the regret until the first entrant purchase using the same steps as in equation (
B.8
) in the proof of Lemma
B.4
(Appendix
B.8
